立刻，在出神之中，我被携过陆地与海洋，来到某个遥远的国度，安放在一辆凯旋车上，同伴们个个头戴桂冠。聚拢的午夜之黑暗，覆盖着全地，把那些正以我们为中心、躁动不宁地织动着的人山人海掩住了：我们听得见他们，却看不见他们。一桩消息，在一小时之前抵达了，其壮丽足与诸世纪相衡量；它太充满了哀婉，太充满了喜悦，除借眼泪、借不息的颂歌、借自大地的唱诗班与乐队回荡而来的感恩颂（Te Deum）之外，再无别的语言可以道出。坐在桂冠车上的我们，蒙有这样的特权：向万国宣告这些消息。而我们的怒马——它们不识恐惧，不识血肉的疲倦——已经借黑暗中听得见的征兆，借喷鼻与踏蹄，责备我们的迟延了。我们*为何*迟延？我们在等一个密语，它该为万国的希望——如今已永远成就的希望——作见证。午夜时分，密语到了；那密语便是——*滑铁卢与失而复得的基督教世界！*那可畏的密语以自身的光闪耀着；它行在我们前头；高骑在我们辕马的头顶之上，在我们所经的路径上铺开金色的光。每一座城，在那密语临现之时，都敞开了城门。江河在我们渡越时也自知觉。诸森林，在我们沿其边缘疾驰时，都因向那密语致敬而战栗。黑暗也领会了它。

午夜后两点钟，我们驶近一座巍峨的大教堂。它那高耸入云的大门紧闭着。但当那骑行在我们前头的可畏密语携着金光抵达时，门便无声地退回铰链之上；我们的车马在飞驰的疾驰之中，进入大教堂恢宏的侧廊。我们的步伐是俯冲的；每一座祭坛之前，在我们行进左右的小礼拜堂与祈祷室里，将熄的、病笃的灯，都应和着那飞掠而过的密语，重新燃起。
===Original===
Forty leagues we might have run in the cathedral, and as yet no strength of morning light had reached us, when before us we saw the aerial galleries of organ and choir. Every pinnacle of fretwork, every station of advantage amongst the traceries, was crested by white-robed choristers that sang deliverance; that wept no more tears, as once their fathers had wept; but at intervals that sang together to the generations, saying,

> “Chant the deliverer’s praise in every tongue,”

and receiving answers from afar,

> “Such as once in heaven and earth were sung.”

And of their chanting was no end; of our headlong pace was neither pause nor slackening. Thus as we ran like torrents—thus as we swept with bridal rapture over the Campo Santo41 of the cathedral graves—suddenly we became aware of a vast necropolis rising upon the far-off horizon—a city of sepulchres, built within the saintly cathedral for the warrior dead that rested from their feuds on Earth. Of purple granite was the necropolis; yet, in the first minute, it lay like a purple stain upon the horizon, so mighty was the distance. In the second minute it trembled through many changes, growing into terraces and towers of wondrous altitude, so mighty was the pace. In the third minute already, with our dreadful gallop, we were entering its suburbs. Vast sarcophagi rose on every side, having towers and turrets that, upon the limits of the central aisle, strode forward with haughty intrusion, that ran back with mighty shadows into answering recesses. Every sarcophagus showed many bas-reliefs—bas-reliefs of battles and of battlefields; battles from forgotten ages, battles from yesterday; battlefields that, long since, nature had healed and reconciled to herself with the sweet oblivion of flowers; battlefields that were yet angry and crimson with carnage. Where the terraces ran, there did *we* run; where the towers curved, there did *we* curve. With the flight of swallows our horses swept round every angle. Like rivers in flood wheeling round headlands, like hurricanes that ride into the secrets of forests, faster than ever light unwove the mazes of darkness, our flying equipage carried earthly passions, kindled warrior instincts, amongst the dust that lay around us—dust oftentimes of our noble fathers that had slept in God from Crécy to Trafalgar.

===Chinese===
四十里格，我们或许已在大教堂里奔驰，而晨光的气力还未曾追及我们，这时在我们前面，出现了管风琴与唱诗班凌空的楼廊。透雕细工的每一座尖顶，花饰窗格间的每一处形胜之地，都冠着身穿白袍的唱诗班歌手，他们唱着救恩；他们不再流泪，像他们祖辈曾流过的那样；而时断时续地，他们一同向着诸世代歌唱，说，

> “用万邦的语言同唱拯救者的赞美，”

并远远地听见应答，

> “正如从前在天上与地上所唱过的那样。”

他们的歌唱没有穷尽；我们的俯冲之速，也没有一息停顿与迟缓。我们就这样如洪流般奔驰——就这样怀着新嫁娘的狂喜，掠过大教堂墓园（Campo Santo）（41）之上——蓦然间，我们觉察到一座浩大的陵城在远方的地平线上升起——一座坟墓之城，建在圣洁的大教堂之内，为那些在地上息了纷争的战士亡者而筑。陵城是紫花岗岩的；然而在第一分钟里，它平铺在地平线上，如一抹紫色的渍痕，距离是那样浩远。第二分钟里，它颤动着经历千变，长成层层台地与巍峨入云的塔楼，速度是那样浩疾。第三分钟里，我们已随我们可怖的疾驰进入它的城郊。巨大的石棺在四面升起，带着塔楼与角塔，在中廊的边界上昂然前突，侵凌逼人，又以巨大的暗影退入应和的龛穴。每具石棺都饰着许多浮雕——战斗与战场的浮雕；有遗忘的年代的战斗，有昨日的战斗；有早已被自然治愈、借花朵甜美的忘却与她和好的战场；也有至今犹带怒色、染着屠杀之红的战场。台地延展到哪里，*我们*便奔驰到哪里；塔楼弯曲到哪里，*我们*便转弯到哪里。我们那飞驰的车马，以燕子的疾飞扫过每一处转角。如洪水中回旋于岬角的江河，如闯入森林奥秘的飓风，比光解开黑暗的迷阵更快，我们飞驰的车马在人间的诸般激情之中穿行，在四围横陈的尘埃之间点燃战士的本能——那尘埃，往往便是我们高贵的祖辈的尘埃，他们自克雷西（Crécy）到特拉法尔加，在上帝里面长眠。
===Original===
And now had we reached the last sarcophagus, now were we abreast of the last bas-relief, already had we recovered the arrow-like flight of the illimitable central aisle, when coming up this aisle to meet us we beheld afar off a female child, that rode in a carriage as frail as flowers. The mists which went before her hid the fawns that drew her, but could not hide the shells and tropic flowers with which she played—but could not hide the lovely smiles by which she uttered her trust in the mighty cathedral, and in the cherubim that looked down upon her from the mighty shafts of its pillars. Face to face she was meeting us; face to face she rode, as if danger there were none. “Oh, baby!” I exclaimed, “shalt thou be the ransom for Waterloo? Must we, that carry tidings of great joy to every people, be messengers of ruin to thee!” In horror I rose at the thought; but then also, in horror at the thought, rose one that was sculptured on a bas-relief—a Dying Trumpeter. Solemnly from the field of battle he rose to his feet; and, unslinging his stony trumpet, carried it, in his dying anguish, to his stony lips—sounding once, and yet once again; proclamation that, in *thy* ears, oh baby! spoke from the battlements of death. Immediately deep shadows fell between us, and aboriginal silence. The choir had ceased to sing. The hoofs of our horses, the dreadful rattle of our harness, the groaning of our wheels, alarmed the graves no more. By horror the bas-relief had been unlocked unto life. By horror we, that were so full of life, we men and our horses, with their fiery forelegs rising in mid air to their everlasting gallop, were frozen to a bas-relief. Then a third time the trumpet sounded; the seals were taken off all pulses; life, and the frenzy of life, tore into their channels again; again the choir burst forth in sunny grandeur, as from the muffling of storms and darkness; again the thunderings of our horses carried temptation into the graves. One cry burst from our lips, as the clouds, drawing off from the aisle, showed it empty before us.—“Whither has the infant fled?—is the young child caught up to God?”

===Chinese===
此刻我们已抵最后一具石棺，此刻我们已与最后一块浮雕并行，此刻我们已然恢复那无际中廊箭一般的疾飞，忽见顺着这中廊迎面而来的远方，一个女孩子，乘坐着一辆柔弱如花的马车。行在她前面的迷雾遮住了拉车的幼鹿，却遮不住她玩耍的贝壳与热带的花朵——遮不住她可爱的微笑，她借以向那巍峨的大教堂、向自巨大柱身的顶端俯视她的基路伯，说出她的信靠。她面对面地迎着我们来；面对面地驶着，仿佛那里并无危险。「哦，婴孩！」我叫道，「难道要你作滑铁卢的赎价？我们这些向万民传报大喜信息的人，难道要做报丧的使者给你！」我惊骇得直起身来；但随之，也在对那念头的惊骇之中，立起了一个刻在浮雕上的人——一个垂死的号手。他庄严地自战场的地上立起身来；把他石头的号角从肩上解下，在垂死的苦痛中举到他石头的唇边——吹响一声，又再吹响一声；那宣告，在*你的*耳中，哦婴孩！是自死亡的雉堞上发出的。顷刻间，深深的暗影降在我们之间，回到太初的寂静。唱诗班止息了歌声。我们马匹的蹄声，我们挽具可怖的辚辚，我们车轮的呻吟，不再惊动坟墓。借着惊骇，那浮雕被解锁而入于生命；借着惊骇，我们这些满溢着生命的——我们人与我们的马，连同它们那为着永恒疾驰而扬在半空的前蹄——也被冻成了一座浮雕。继而，第三次，号角吹响；一切脉搏上的封印被解除了；生命，与生命的狂澜，重新冲入它的河道；唱诗班再度迸发出晴日的庄严，仿佛自风暴与黑暗的裹覆中挣出；我们马匹的雷鸣再度把试探载进坟墓。一声呼喊从我们唇间迸出，其时云朵正从侧廊上退开，把它空空地呈现在我们面前。——「婴孩逃到哪里去了？——那小孩子是否已被提到上帝那里？」
===Original===
Lo! afar off, in a vast recess, rose three mighty windows to the clouds; and on a level with their summits, at height insuperable to man, rose an altar of purest alabaster. On its eastern face was trembling a crimson glory. A glory was it from the reddening dawn that now streamed *through* the windows? Was it from the crimson robes of the martyrs painted *on* the windows? Was it from the bloody bas-reliefs of Earth? There, suddenly, within that crimson radiance, rose the apparition of a woman’s head, and then of a woman’s figure. The child it was—grown up to woman’s height. Clinging to the horns of the altar, voiceless she stood—sinking, rising, raving, despairing; and behind the volume of incense that, night and day, streamed upwards from the altar, dimly was seen the fiery font, and the shadow of that dreadful being who should have baptized her with the baptism of death. But by her side was kneeling her better angel, that hid his face with wings; that wept and pleaded for *her*; that prayed when *she* could *not*; that fought with Heaven by tears for *her* deliverance; which also, as he raised his immortal countenance from his wings, I saw, by the glory in his eye, that from Heaven he had won at last.

===Chinese===
看哪！远方，在一处浩大的龛室里，三扇巨窗升起，直抵云霄；与窗顶齐平的地方，在人不可企及的高处，升起一座至纯雪花石的祭坛。它的东面颤动着一片绯红的荣光。那荣光，可是来自此刻*透过*窗户倾泻的渐红的黎明？可是来自*绘在*窗上的殉道者的绯红袍服？可是来自大地上那些血染的浮雕？就在那里，忽然，在那绯红的光辉之中，升起一个妇人的头的显现，继而是一个妇人的身形。正是那孩子——已长成妇人的身量。她紧攀着祭坛的角，无声地立着——沉落，昂起，谵狂，绝望；而在那自祭坛昼夜上腾的香云之后，隐约可见那火红的洗礼盆，与那可怖存在者的暗影——本该是他以死亡之洗礼为她施洗的。但在她身旁，跪着她的良善天使，他以翅膀掩着脸；他为*她*哭泣，为*她*恳求；*她*不能祷告时，*他*代祷；他以眼泪同上天角力，求*她*的释放；而当他从翅膀上抬起他不朽的面容时，我从他眼中的荣光看见：他终于从上天赢得了它。

#### V / 五

===Original===
Then was completed the passion of the mighty fugue. The golden tubes of the organ, which as yet had but muttered at intervals—gleaming amongst clouds and surges of incense—threw up, as from fountains unfathomable, columns of heart-shattering music. Choir and anti-choir were filling fast with unknown voices. Thou also, Dying Trumpeter, with thy love that was victorious, and thy anguish that was finishing, didst enter the tumult; trumpet and echo—farewell love, and farewell anguish—rang through the dreadful *sanctus*. Oh, darkness of the grave! that from the crimson altar and from the fiery font wert visited and searched by the effulgence in the angel’s eye—were these indeed thy children? Pomps of life, that, from the burials of centuries, rose again to the voice of perfect joy, did ye indeed mingle with the festivals of Death? Lo! as I looked back for seventy leagues through the mighty cathedral, I saw the quick and the dead that sang together to God, together that sang to the generations of man. All the hosts of jubilation, like armies that ride in pursuit, moved with one step. Us, that, with laurelled heads, were passing from the cathedral, they overtook, and, as with a garment, they wrapped us round with thunders greater than our own. As brothers we moved together; to the dawn that advanced, to the stars that fled; rendering thanks to God in the highest—that, having hid His face through one generation behind thick clouds of War, once again was ascending, from the Campo Santo of Waterloo was ascending, in the visions of Peace; rendering thanks for thee, young girl! whom having overshadowed with His ineffable passion of death, suddenly did God relent, suffered thy angel to turn aside His arm, and even in thee, sister unknown! shown to me for a moment only to be hidden forever, found an occasion to glorify His goodness. A thousand times, amongst the phantoms of sleep, have I seen thee entering the gates of the golden dawn, with the secret word riding before thee, with the armies of the grave behind thee—seen thee sinking, rising, raving, despairing; a thousand times in the worlds of sleep have I seen thee followed by God’s angel through storms, through desert seas, through the darkness of quicksands, through dreams and the dreadful revelations that are in dreams; only that at the last, with one sling of His victorious arm, He might snatch thee back from ruin, and might emblazon in thy deliverance the endless resurrections of His love!

===Chinese===
于是，那阕浩大赋格的受难始得完成。风琴的金管——它们此前只在间歇中低语，在香云与香涛之间闪烁——此刻自不可测的泉源，喷射出摧心裂肺的音乐之柱。唱诗班与对唱诗班，迅速被无从识认的嗓音充满。你也是啊，垂死的号手，带着你得胜的爱、你那正在终结的苦痛，进入了这场喧腾；号角与回声——别了，爱；别了，苦痛——在可畏的*圣哉*（*sanctus*）声中回荡。哦，坟墓的黑暗啊！你曾自那绯红的祭坛、自那火红的洗礼盆，被天使眼中的光华造访、被它搜寻——这些当真是你的儿女么？生命的仪仗啊，你们自百世的葬列中，重新升起到那全然喜乐的声音里，当真曾同死亡的节庆混在一处么？看哪！我回头望过大教堂七十里格的纵深，我看见活人与死人一同向上帝歌唱，一同向人的诸世代歌唱。欢乐的全部军旅，如追奔的劲旅，踏着同一个步伐行进。我们——头戴桂冠、正欲出教堂而去的我们——被他们赶上，他们如披一件衣裳，以大于我们自己的雷鸣把我们包裹起来。我们如手足同行；向着推进的黎明，向着奔逃的星辰；向至高处的上帝献上感谢——他，曾借厚厚的战争之云把他的脸掩藏了一代之久，此刻正重新升起，自滑铁卢的教堂墓园升起，在和平的诸异象中升起；也为你献上感谢，年轻的姑娘！他曾以他那不可言宣的死亡之激情荫蔽了你，却蓦然回心转意，容让你的天使拨转他的手臂，并且就在你身上——无名的姊妹啊！只显现给我片刻、便要永远隐藏的姊妹——找到了一个荣耀他良善的机会。一千次，在睡眠的幻影之间，我见过你走进金色黎明的门，那密语骑行在你的前头，坟墓的军旅跟在你的身后——见过你沉落，昂起，谵狂，绝望；一千次，在睡眠的世界里，我见过上帝的天使追随着你，穿过风暴，穿过荒凉的海，穿过流沙的黑暗，穿过梦与梦中那些可怖的启示；只为着到末了，他那只得胜的手臂一挥，把你从劫难中夺回，并在你的得救之上，彰显他之爱那无止息的复活！

## The Palimpsest of the Human Brain / 人脑的重写本

===Original===
You know perhaps, masculine reader, better than I can tell you, what is a “Palimpsest.” Possibly you have one in your own library. But yet, for the sake of others who may not know, or may have forgotten, suffer me to explain it here, lest any female reader who honours these papers with her notice should tax me with explaining it once too seldom; which would be worse to bear than a simultaneous complaint from twelve proud men that I had explained it three times too often. You, therefore, fair reader, understand that for *your* accommodation exclusively I explain the meaning of this word. It is Greek; and our sex enjoys the office and privilege of standing counsel to yours in all questions of Greek. We are, under favour, perpetual and hereditary dragomans to you. So that if, by accident, you know the meaning of a Greek word, yet by courtesy to us, your counsel learned in that matter, you will always seem *not* to know it.

A palimpsest, then, is a membrane or roll cleansed of its manuscript by reiterated successions.

===Chinese===
男性读者，至于「重写本」（palimpsest）是什么，你或许比我所能告诉你的知道得更清楚；你的藏书里兴许就有一部。然而，为了那些不知道、或已经忘却的人，且容我在此解释一番，免得任何垂青披阅这些篇章的女性读者责我少解释了一次；那样一种委屈，比十二位傲慢的先生齐声抱怨我多解释了三次，还要难以忍受。所以，秀美的读者，你要明白：全然是为了*你的*方便，我才来解释这个词的含义。它是希腊文；而在一切希腊文的问题上，我们男性享有为你们女性担任常任顾问律师的职分与特权。恕我僭越地说，我们是你们永久世袭的通译（dragoman）。因此，倘若你碰巧认得某个希腊词的意思，出于对我们——你们精通此道的顾问——的礼敬，你也总要在表面上装作不知道它。

那么，所谓重写本，便是一幅皮膜或一卷纸卷，其上的字迹经一再反复的洗刷而被涤除。

===Original===
What was the reason that the Greeks and the Romans had not the advantage of printed books? The answer will be, from ninety-nine persons in a hundred—Because the mystery of printing was not then discovered. But this is altogether a mistake. The secret of printing must have been discovered many thousands of times before it was used, or *could* be used. The inventive powers of man are divine; and also his stupidity is divine, as Cowper so playfully illustrates in the slow development of the sofa through successive generations of immortal dullness. It took centuries of blockheads to raise a joint stool into a chair; and it required something like a miracle of genius, in the estimate of elder generations, to reveal the possibility of lengthening a chair into a *chaise-longue*, or a sofa. Yes, these were inventions that cost mighty throes of intellectual power. But still, as respects printing, and admirable as is the stupidity of man, it was really not quite equal to the task of evading an object which stared him in the face with so broad a gaze. It did not require an Athenian intellect to read the main secret of printing in many scores of processes which the ordinary uses of life were *daily* repeating. To say nothing of analogous artifices amongst various mechanic artisans, all that is essential in printing must have been known to every nation that struck coins and medals. Not, therefore, any want of a printing art—that is, of an art for multiplying impressions—but the want of a cheap material for *receiving* such impressions, was the obstacle to an introduction of printed books even as early as Pisistratus. The ancients did apply printing to records of silver and gold; to marble, and many other substances cheaper than gold or silver, they did *not*, since each monument required a *separate* effort of inscription. Simply this defect it was of a cheap material for receiving impresses which froze in its very fountains the early resources of printing.

===Chinese===
希腊人和罗马人为何不曾享有印刷书籍之利？百分之九十九的人都会回答说——因为那时印刷术的秘密尚未被发现。但这全然是一个错误。在印刷术得到应用、或*能够*得到应用之前，它的秘密必定已被发现过千万次。人的发明才能是神圣的；人的愚钝同样是神圣的——科珀（Cowper）便曾以不朽的愚钝之世代更迭中沙发那迟缓的成形史，如此妙趣横生地例示过这一点。把一张坐墩抬升为一把椅子，耗去了几个世纪的蠢材；而在先辈世代的估量里，要把一把椅子接长为一张 *chaise-longue*（躺椅）或一张沙发，则几乎需要天才的奇迹。是啊，这些都是耗费了智力巨痛的发明。然而，就印刷而论，人的愚钝纵然可惊可叹，却终究还不甚有本事躲开那个以如此直瞪瞪的目光逼视着人的目标。要在日常生活*日日*重复的几十道工序里读出印刷术的主要秘密，本不需要什么雅典人式的才智。且不论各行工匠中间种种类似的机巧——凡铸造过钱币与勋章的民族，必定早已通晓印刷术中一切本质的东西。因此，阻碍印刷书籍早在庇西特拉图（Pisistratus）的时代乃至更早就问世的东西，并不是缺少一门印刷之术——即复制印痕之术——而是缺少一种*承印*这类印痕的廉价材料。古人确曾把印刷施于金银之上的铭刻；至于大理石，以及许多较金银为贱的其他材料，他们却*不曾*施以印刷，因为每一座碑碣都需要一次*单独*的镌刻之功。正是这一受印廉价材料的缺欠，把印刷术早期的资源在其源头处便冻结住了。

===Original===
Some twenty years ago this view of the case was luminously expounded by Dr. Whately, and with the merit, I believe, of having first suggested it. Since then, this theory has received indirect confirmation. Now, out of that original scarcity affecting all materials proper for durable books, which continued up to times comparatively modern, grew the opening for palimpsests. Naturally, when once a roll of parchment or of vellum had done its office, by propagating through a series of generations what once had possessed an interest for *them*, but which, under changes of opinion or of taste, had faded to their feelings or had become obsolete for their undertakings, the whole *membrana* or vellum skin, the twofold product of human skill and costly material, and the costly freight of thought which it carried, drooped in value concurrently—supposing that each were inalienably associated to the other. Once it had been the impress of a human mind which stamped its value upon the vellum; the vellum, though costly, had contributed but a secondary element of value to the total result. At length, however, this relation between the vehicle and its freight has gradually been undermined. The vellum, from having been the setting of the jewel, has risen at length to be the jewel itself; and the burden of thought, from having given the chief value to the vellum, has now become the chief obstacle to its value; nay, has totally extinguished its value, unless it can be dissociated from the connection. Yet, if this unlinking *can* be effected, then, fast as the inscription upon the membrane is sinking into rubbish, the membrane itself is reviving in its separate importance; and, from bearing a ministerial value, the vellum has come at last to absorb the whole value.

===Chinese===
大约二十年以前，惠特利博士（Dr. Whately）曾把这番见解阐发得透辟入里，而且我相信，还兼具首创之功。自那以后，这一理论已得到间接的印证。当初，凡宜于充任耐久书籍的材料无不陷于匮乏，这种原初的匮乏一直延续到较为晚近的时代；重写本的门径，正是从这匮乏之中生长出来的。理所当然地，一卷羊皮纸或犊皮纸一旦尽了它的职分——把它那曾经*于他们*别有意味的内容传布给了一连串世代之后，这些内容在意见或趣味的变迁之下，于他们的情感已归淡漠，于他们的事业已成废弃——于是，整幅 *membrana*（拉丁语：皮膜），即那张犊皮，这人类技艺与昂贵材料的双重产儿，连同它所载运的那贵重的思想之货，其价值便一并跌落了——这里假定膜与文二者牢不可分地绑在一处。曾几何时，是人心压下的印迹把价值钤盖在犊皮之上；犊皮虽贵，在总的结果中不过贡献了次要的价值成分。然而终究，载体与其货物之间的这层关系渐渐被掘空了。犊皮从珠宝的座垫，终于升为珠宝本身；思想的负载，从赋予犊皮以主要价值的东西，变成了它价值的主要障碍；非但如此，除非能把思想从这份联结中拆解出来，它已把犊皮的价值全然扑灭。然而，这解链之功若*确能*成就，那么，膜上的铭文愈是急速地沉沦为废屑，膜本身便愈是在它独立的紧要性中回苏；犊皮从承载一种辅佐的价值，到头来竟把整个价值都吸收了去。

===Original===
Hence the importance for our ancestors that the separation *should* be effected. Hence it arose in the Middle Ages as a considerable object for chemistry to discharge the writing from the roll, and thus to make it available for a new succession of thoughts. The soil, if cleansed from what once had been hothouse plants, but now were held to be weeds, would be ready to receive a fresh and more appropriate crop. In that object the monkish chemists succeeded; but after a fashion which seems almost incredible—incredible not as regards the extent of their success, but as regards the delicacy of restraints under which it moved—so equally adjusted was their success to the immediate interests of that period, and to the reversionary objects of our own. They did the thing; but not so radically as to prevent us, their posterity, from *un*doing it. They expelled the writing sufficiently to leave a field for the new manuscript, and yet not sufficiently to make the traces of the elder manuscript irrecoverable for us. Could magic, could Hermes Trismegiatus, have done more? What would you think, fair reader, of a problem such as this:—to write a book which should be sense for your own generation, nonsense for the next; should revive into sense for the next after that, but again become nonsense for the fourth; and so on by alternate successions sinking into night or blazing into day, like the Sicilian river Arethusa and the English river Mole, or like the undulating motions of a flattened stone which children cause to skim the breast of a river, now diving below the water, now grazing its surface, sinking heavily into darkness, rising buoyantly into light, through a long vista of alternations? Such a problem, you say, is impossible. But really it is a problem not harder apparently than to bid a generation kill, so that a subsequent generation may call back into life; bury, so that posterity may command to rise again. Yet *that* was what the rude chemistry of past ages effected when coming into combination with the reaction from the more refined chemistry of our own. Had *they* been better chemists, had *we* been worse, the mixed result—namely, that, dying for *them*, the flower should revive for *us*—could not have been effected. They did the thing proposed to them: they did it effectually, for they founded upon it all that was wanted: and yet ineffectually, since we unravelled their work—effacing all above which they had superscribed, restoring all below which they had effaced.

===Chinese===
因此，对我们的祖先而言，这分离之*务须*成就，是何等重大。于是在中世纪，把字迹从纸卷上涤除、使它得供一系新思想之用，便蔚然成为化学的一桩可观事业。这片土壤，一经洗净了那些昔日曾为温房嘉卉、如今却被视作莠草的东西，便可准备领受一茬更新鲜、更相宜的庄稼。在这一目标上，僧侣化学家们成功了；但其成功的方式却近乎令人难以置信——难以置信，不在其成功之广，而在其运行所受约束之精微：他们的成功，与那个时代的眼前利益、与我们自己时代的复归之利，竟调配得如此均停。他们做成了这件事；却做得不够根除，未能阻止我们这些后裔去*解*开它。他们把字迹涤除得恰够为新抄本留出一片田地，却又未涤除到使旧抄本的踪迹于我们成为无从追复。纵是魔法，纵是赫尔墨斯·特里斯梅吉斯托斯（Hermes Trismegiatus），还能做得更多么？秀美的读者，对于这样一道难题，你会作何感想：——写一部书，它于自己的一代为有意义，于下一代为无意义；于再下一代又复苏为有意义，于第四代复又沦为无意义；如此交替相续，一时沉入黑夜，一时炽然燃入白昼，如西西里的阿瑞图萨河（Arethusa）与英格兰的莫尔河（Mole），又如孩童掷向河胸、贴水掠飞的扁石，一起一伏：忽而潜入水下，忽而擦过水面，沉沉坠入幽暗，翩翩升入光明，在悠长的一串明灭交替里跌宕不尽？你会说，这样的难题是不可能成立的。可是说真的，这道题看上去并不比下面这道更难：吩咐一个世代去杀死，好让后继的世代能唤回生命；去掩埋，好让子孙后代能号令死者复起。然而，往昔粗糙的化学一经与我们今日更精纯的化学的反作用相会合，所成就的恰恰*就是这个*。倘若*他们*的化学更高明些，*我们*的更拙劣些，那复合的成果——即花朵*于他们*死去，却*于我们*复活——便无从达成了。交给他们的差事，他们办到了：他们办得有实效，因为凡所需用的，他们尽都奠基在这上面；却又办得无实效，因为我们拆解了他们的工作——把他们在上面所加写的一切抹去，把他们在下面所抹去的一切复原。

===Original===
Here, for instance, is a parchment which contained some Grecian tragedy—the Agamemnon of Aeschylus, or the Phoenissae of Euripides. This had possessed a value almost inappreciable in the eyes of accomplished scholars, continually growing rarer through generations. But four centuries are gone by since the destruction of the Western Empire. Christianity, with towering grandeurs of another class, has founded a different empire; and some bigoted, yet perhaps holy monk has washed away (as he persuades himself) the heathen’s tragedy, replacing it with a monastic legend; which legend is disfigured with fables in its incidents, and yet in a higher sense is true, because interwoven with Christian morals, and with the sublimest of Christian revelations. Three, four, five, centuries more find man still devout as ever; but the language has become obsolete; and even for Christian devotion a new era has arisen, throwing it into the channel of crusading zeal or of chivalrous enthusiasm. The *membrana* is wanted now for a knightly romance—for *My Cid* or Coeur de Lion, for Sir Tristrem or Lybaeus Disconus. In this way, by means of the imperfect chemistry known to the medieval period, the same roll has served as a conservatory for three separate generations of flowers and fruits, all perfectly different, and yet all specially adapted to the wants of the successive possessors. The Greek tragedy, the monkish legend, the knightly romance, each has ruled its own period. One harvest after another has been gathered into the garners of man through ages far apart. And the same hydraulic machinery has distributed, through the same marble fountains, water, milk, or wine, according to the habits and training of the generations that came to quench their thirst.

===Chinese===
譬如，这里有一幅羊皮纸，上面原本写着某部希腊悲剧——埃斯库罗斯（Aeschylus）的《阿伽门农》（Agamemnon），或是欧里庇得斯（Euripides）的《腓尼基妇女》（Phoenissae）。在造诣精深的学者眼中，它曾拥有一种几乎无从估量的价值，且随世代推移而愈见珍稀。然而自西部帝国覆亡以来，四个世纪已然流逝。基督教携着另一等巍峨的壮丽，缔造了一个不同的帝国；某位偏执、却也或许圣洁的修士，把异教的悲剧洗刷了去（至少他这样说服自己），代之以一篇僧院传奇；这传奇在其情节上不免为荒谈怪语所玷，在更高的意义上却是真实的，因为它与基督教的道德、与基督教最崇高的启示交织为一。再过三百年、四百年、五百年，人依然一如往昔地虔敬；但语言已经陈旧；甚至对基督教的虔敬而言，一个新的时代也已兴起，把它导入十字军的热忱、或骑士式的激情那条河道。如今人们需要这幅 *membrana* 来承载一部骑士传奇——写熙德（My Cid）或狮心王（Coeur de Lion），写《特里斯特拉姆爵士》（Sir Tristrem）或《利贝乌斯·迪斯科努斯》（Lybaeus Disconus）。就这样，凭着中世纪所知的并不完善的化学，同一卷纸先后充当了三代花草果实的温室：三代彼此全然不同，却又各自恰合于相继持有者的需要。希腊悲剧、僧院传奇、骑士传奇，各自主宰了自己的时期。一茬又一茬的收获，隔着迢遥的岁月，先后归入人类的谷仓。而同一套水利机括，透过同一座大理石泉口，依照前来解渴的诸世代的习尚与教养，或分发清水，或分发乳汁，或分发醇酒。

===Original===
Such were the achievements of rude monastic chemistry. But the more elaborate chemistry of our own days has reversed all these motions of our simple ancestors, with results in every stage that to *them* would have real realised the most fantastic amongst the promises of thaumaturgy. Insolent vaunt of Paracelsus, that he would restore the original rose or violet out of the ashes settling from its combustion—that is now rivalled in this modern achievement. The traces of each successive handwriting, regularly effaced, as had been imagined, have, in the inverse order, been regularly called back; the footsteps of the game pursued, wolf or stag, in each several chase, have been unlinked, and hunted back through all their doubles; and, as the chorus of the Athenian stage unwove through the antistrophe every step that had been mystically woven through the strophe, so, by our modern conjurations of science, secrets of ages remote from each other have been exorcised42 from the accumulated shadows of centuries. Chemistry, a witch as potent as the Erictho of Lucan (*Pharsalia*, lib. VI or VII), has extorted by her torments, from the dust and ashes of forgotten centuries, the secrets of a life extinct for the general eye, but still glowing in the embers. Even the fable of the Phoenix, that secular bird who propagated his solitary existence, and his solitary births, along the line of centuries, through eternal relays of funeral mists, is but a type of what we have done with Palimpsests. We have backed upon each phoenix in the long *regressus*, and forced him to expose his ancestral phoenix, sleeping in the ashes below his own ashes. Our good old forefathers would have been aghast at our sorceries; and, if they speculated on the propriety of burning Dr. Faustus, *us* they would have burned by acclamation. Trial there would have been none; and they could not otherwise have satisfied their horror of the brazen profligacy marking our modern magic than by ploughing up the houses of all who had been parties to it, and sowing the ground with salt.

===Chinese===
这便是粗糙的僧院化学的种种业绩。而我们今日更为精微的化学，则把先辈那种朴素的运作尽数倒了转来；其每一阶段的结果，若置于*他们*眼中，都足真真切切地实现奇迹术诸般许诺中最奇幻的一条。帕拉塞尔苏斯（Paracelsus）狂倨地自诩，说他能从玫瑰或紫罗兰燃烬沉落的灰里，把原先那朵玫瑰或紫罗兰复原出来——在这项现代的成就里，此语如今已有人与之比肩。相继各层字迹的踪迹，人们原以为已被规规矩矩地抹去，如今却依着相反的次序，规规矩矩地被唤了回来；每一场围逐中被追逐的猎物——狼也好，牡鹿也好——其足踪已被解链解开，循着一切盘绕的折线被猎溯回去；正如雅典舞台上的歌队，经由返歌（antistrophe）把先前经正歌（strophe）神秘织入的每一步舞步逐一解织，同样地，凭着我们现代科学的咒召，彼此悬隔的世代之谜，已从数百年沉积的暗影中被攘除而出（42）。化学，一位法力不逊于卢坎（Lucan）《法沙利亚》（*Pharsalia*）第六或第七卷里厄里克托（Erictho）的女巫，已经用她的酷刑，从被遗忘诸世纪的尘与灰中，逼出了那在常人眼中早已断绝、却仍在余烬里幽幽发亮的生命之秘密。乃至凤凰的寓言——那只绵亘世纪的长生之鸟，穿过一程接一程永不断绝的葬仪雾霭，沿着世纪的线索传衍它孤独的存在与孤独的诞生——也不过是我们在重写本上之所为的一个预型。在漫长的 *regressus*（逆溯）里，我们逐一贴到每一只凤凰的背后，逼它交出它祖辈的那一只——正睡在它自身灰烬之下的灰烬里。我们那些善良的老祖宗，见了我们的巫术定要骇然失色；倘若他们议论过烧死浮士德博士（Dr. Faustus）是否允当，那么轮到*我们*，他们必会齐声喝彩，把我们付之一炬。审判是决计不会有的；我们现代魔法带着那副厚颜无耻的放荡相，他们心中这份惊骇，若不把凡与此事有牵连者的屋宅尽数犁翻、往地里撒上盐，便无论如何也无从餍足。

===Original===
Fancy not, reader, that this tumult of images, illustrative or allusive, moves under any impulse or purpose of mirth. It is but the coruscation of a restless understanding, often made ten times more so by irritation of the nerves, such as you will first learn to comprehend (its *how* and its *why*) some stage or two ahead. The image, the memorial, the record, which for me is derived from a palimpsest as to one great fact in our human being, and which immediately I will show you, is but too repellent of laughter; or, even if laughter *had* been possible, it would have been such laughter as oftentimes is thrown off from the fields of ocean43—laughter that hides, or that seems to evade, mustering tumult; foam-bells that weave garlands of phosphoric radiance for one moment round the eddies of gleaming abysses; mimicries of earthborn flowers that for the eye raise phantoms of gaiety, as oftentimes for the ear they raise the echoes of fugitive laughter, mixing with the ravings and choir-voices of an angry sea.

What else than a natural and mighty palimpsest is the human brain? Such a palimpsest is my brain; such a palimpsest, oh reader! is yours. Everlasting layers of ideas, images, feelings, have fallen upon your brain softly as light. Each succession has seemed to bury all that went before. And yet, in reality, not one has been extinguished. And, if in the vellum palimpsest, lying amongst the oilier *diplomata* of human archives or libraries, there is anything fantastic or which moves to laughter, as oftentimes there is in the grotesque collisions of those successive themes, having no natural connection, which by pure accident have consecutively occupied the roll, yet, in our own heaven-created palimpsest, the deep memorial palimpsest of the brain, there are not and cannot be such incoherences. The fleeting accidents of a man’s life, and its external shows, may indeed be irrelate and incongruous; but the organising principles which fuse into harmony, and gather about fixed predetermined centres, whatever heterogeneous elements life may have accumulated from without, will not permit the grandeur of human unity greatly to be violated, or its ultimate repose to be troubled, in the retrospect from dying moments, or from other great convulsions.

===Chinese===
读者，切勿以为这番意象的纷扰骚动——无论其为阐释的还是暗指的——是在任何快意的冲动或目的之下运行的。它不过是一颗不得安歇的知性迸射的电光；神经受激，又常常使它十倍地如此。至于其中的*何以*与*何故*，你要再前行一两个阶段，方能初初学会领会。那意象、那纪念、那记录——它是就我们人类存在中的一件重大事实，向我从一部重写本里取来的，我随即便要指给你看——它实在过于拒斥笑声了；或者，纵使笑声*本来*可能，那也必是这样的笑声：即时常从海洋的田野（43）上迸散的那种——那笑声遮掩着，或者似乎闪避着，正在集结的骚动；是一朵朵浪沫的铃花，霎时间在闪光深渊的涡流四围编结磷光的花环；是土生花朵的摹像，为眼睛升起欢愉的幻影，正如它们时常为耳朵升起逃逸的笑声的回声，混入怒海的呓谵与歌队之声里。

人脑，不是一部天然而雄大的重写本，又能是什么？我的脑便是这样一部重写本；读者啊，你的脑也是这样一部重写本。观念、意象、情感的层层积叠，永不止息，如光一般轻柔地落在你的脑上。每一层相继而来，似乎都把先前的一切尽数掩埋；然而实际上，却没有一层曾被扑灭。倘若在那犊皮的重写本里——它混迹于人类档库或书楼所藏那些油亮的文牒（*diplomata*）之间——尚有什么荒诞的、引人失笑的东西，一如那些相继占据纸卷、彼此并无天然关联、纯出偶然的题旨时常发生的光怪陆离的相撞；那么，在我们这部上天所造的重写本里，在脑这部深藏纪念的重写本里，这般的不谐和是没有的，也是不能有的。人生流转无常的际遇，及其外表的排场，诚然可以互不相关、彼此乖迕；但是那些组织的原则——它们把生命自外部积累的种种异质元素熔铸为和谐，聚拢到固定而预定的中心四周——决不容许人类一体之庄严，在从临终时刻、或从其他大震荡所回望的目光里，遭受重大的玷损，也不容其终极的安宁横遭搅扰。

===Original===
Such a convulsion is the struggle of gradual suffocation, as in drowning; and in the original *Opium Confessions* I mentioned a case of that nature communicated to me by a lady from her own childish experience. The lady was then still living, though of unusually great age; and I may mention that amongst her faults never was numbered any levity of principle, or carelessness of the most scrupulous veracity, but, on the contrary, such faults as arise from austerity, too harsh, perhaps, and gloomy, indulgent neither to others nor herself. And, at the time of relating this incident, when already very old, she had become religious to asceticism. According to my present belief, she had completed her ninth year when, playing by the side of a solitary brook, she fell into one of its deepest pools. Eventually, but after what lapse of time nobody ever knew, she was saved from death by a farmer, who, riding in some distant lane, had seen her rise to the surface; but not until she had descended within the abyss of death and looked into its secrets, as far, perhaps, as ever human eye *can* have looked that had permission to return. At a certain stage of this descent, a blow seemed to strike her; phosphoric radiance sprang forth from her eyeballs; and immediately a mighty theatre expanded within her brain. In a moment, in the twinkling of an eye, every act, every design of her past life, lived again, arraying themselves not as a succession, but as parts of a coexistence. Such a light fell upon the whole path of her life backwards into the shades of infancy as the light, perhaps, which wrapt the destined Apostle on his road to Damascus. Yet that light blinded for a season; but hers poured celestial vision upon the brain, so that her consciousness became omnipresent at one moment to every feature in the infinite review.

===Chinese===
这样的震荡，便是如溺水时那种渐次窒息的挣扎；在最初的那部《鸦片自白》（the original *Opium Confessions*）里，我提到过一个这类性质的案例，是一位女士以她自身幼年的经历告诉我的。那位女士当时尚在人世，虽然年事已高得出奇；我不妨说，在她种种缺点之中，从来不曾列有品格上的轻浮，也从不曾列有对最严格真实的疏忽大意；恰恰相反，她的缺点是从严峻里生出的——也许过于严酷，过于阴沉，无论对别人还是对自己都不肯宽容。而在讲述这段遭遇的时候，已是暮年的她，早已虔敬到苦修的地步。据我如今的信念推算，她刚满九岁那年，在一条僻静的溪流旁玩耍，跌进了溪中最深的水潭之一。最终——但隔了多长的时间，谁也不知道——她被一位农夫从死亡里救了出来；他骑马行在某条远处的小径上，望见她浮上了水面；然而那已是在她降入死亡的深渊之内、窥见了它的秘密之后——也许，一双获准重返人间的凡人之眼，所能窥见的深度，从来也不过如此了。在这次下降的某一段落，仿佛有一击落在她身上；磷光从她的眼球迸射而出；霎时，一座浩大的剧场在她的脑中展开。刹那之间，眨眼之间，她过往生活中的每一个行为、每一个图谋，都重新活了过来；它们的陈列并非前后相续之列，而是共存一体之诸部分。有一种光照在她生命的整条路径上——那路径向后延伸，直入婴儿期的幽影之中——那光，或许正像裹住那位命定的使徒在往大马士革路上的光。然而那光使人一时失明；她的光却把属天的异象倾注于脑中，以致在那无穷回顾里的每一细部之上，她的意识于同一瞬间无所不在。

===Original===
This anecdote was treated sceptically at the time by some critics. But, besides that it has since been confirmed by other experience essentially the same, reported by other parties in the same circumstances, who had never heard of each other, the true point for astonishment is not the *simultaneity* of arrangement under which the past events of life, though in fact successive, had formed their dread line of revelation. This was but a secondary phenomenon; the deeper lay in the resurrection itself, and the possibility of resurrection for what had so long slept in the dust. A pall, deep as oblivion, had been thrown by life over every trace of these experiences; and yet suddenly, at a silent command, at the signal of a blazing rocket sent up from the brain, the pall draws up, and the whole depths of the theatre are exposed. Here was the greater mystery. Now, this mystery is liable to no doubt; for it is repeated, and ten thousand times repeated, by opium, for those who are its martyrs.

Yes, reader, countless are the mysterious handwritings of grief or joy which have inscribed themselves successively upon the palimpsest of your brain; and, like the annual leaves of aboriginal forests, or the undissolving snows on the Himalaya, or light falling upon light, the endless strata have covered up each other in forgetfulness. But by the hour of death, but by fever, but by the searchings of opium, all these can revive in strength. They are not dead, but sleeping. In the illustration imagined by myself from the case of some individual palimpsest, the Grecian tragedy had seemed to be displaced, but was *not* displaced, by the monkish legend; and the monkish legend had seemed to be displaced, but was *not* displaced, by the knightly romance. In some potent convulsion of the system, all wheels back into its earliest elementary stage. The bewildering romance, light tarnished with darkness, the semi-fabulous legend, truth celestial mixed with human falsehoods, these fade even of themselves as life advances. The romance has perished that the young man adored; the legend has gone that deluded the boy; but the deep, deep tragedies of infancy, as when the child’s hands were unlinked forever from his mother’s neck, or his lips forever from his sister’s kisses, these remain lurking below all, and these lurk to the last. Alchemy there is none of passion or disease that can scorch away these immortal impresses; and the dream which closed the preceding section, together with the succeeding dreams of this (which may be viewed as in the nature of choruses winding up the overture contained in Part I), are but illustrations of this truth, such as every man probably will meet experimentally who passes through similar convulsions of dreaming or delirium from any similar or equal disturbance in his nature.44

===Chinese===
这段轶事当年曾遭到某些评论者的怀疑对待。然而，除了它此后已获得印证——一些本质上相同的经历，由处境相同的另一些人所报告，而这些人彼此从未相闻——真正可惊的要点，并不在于那种*同时性*的排布：借着它，人生的往事虽然实际上前后相续，却排成了它们那可畏的启示之线。这不过是次要的现象；更深的一层在于复活本身，在于如此长久睡在尘土里的东西竟还可能有复活。人生曾把一方深沉如遗忘的殓布，罩在这些经历的每一丝痕迹之上；然而忽然之间，随一道无声的号令，随一发从脑中升起的炽亮火箭的信号，殓布揭起，剧场通体的深处都裸露出来。更大的奥秘就在这里。而这奥秘容不得任何怀疑；因为在那些以鸦片为殉道者的人身上，鸦片把这奥秘重复了一遍，又重复了千万遍。

是的，读者，悲哀或欢乐的种种神秘字迹，已经相继铭写在你脑的重写本之上，其数无穷；它们如原始森林里岁岁凋落的树叶，如喜马拉雅山上永不消融的积雪，如光上加落的光，这无穷的地层已在遗忘之中层层相覆。然而单凭死亡的时辰，单凭热病，单凭鸦片的搜检，这一切便能带着全部力量复苏。它们没有死，只是睡着。在我自己就某一部单独的重写本所设想的那个例证里，希腊悲剧似乎被僧院传奇置换了，实则并未被置换；僧院传奇似乎被骑士传奇置换了，实则并未被置换。在机体的某一次强力震荡之中，一切都倒转回它最初的原初阶段。那令人目眩神迷的传奇——光被黑暗磨损了光泽；那半属寓言的传说——属天的真理混着人间的虚妄——随着生命前行，它们甚至自行褪色。青年爱慕过的传奇已经湮灭；蒙骗过孩童的传说已经消逝；然而婴孩时代那些深沉、深沉的悲剧——例如孩子的双手从母亲的颈项上被永远解开、他的嘴唇从姊妹的亲吻上被永远移开的时刻——这些却潜伏在一切之下，而且潜伏到最后。无论激情还是疾病，都没有任何炼金术能把这不朽的印迹烧灼而去；上一篇末尾收束的那个梦，连同本篇中相继而来的诸梦（它们可视为一种合唱，为第一部所含的序曲作结），不过是这一真理的例证罢了；凡在本性中经历过类似或同等的骚乱、从而经受梦与谵妄之类似震荡的人，大约都会以切身的经历与这些例证相遇（44）。

## The Princess Who Overlooked One Seed in a Pomegranate / 漏数石榴籽的公主

===Original===
There is a story told in the *Arabian Nights* of a princess who, by overlooking one seed of a pomegranate, precipitated the event which she had laboured to make impossible. She lies in wait for the event which she foresees. The pomegranate swells, opens, splits; the seeds, which she knows to be roots of evil, rapidly she swallows; but one﻿—only one﻿—before it could be arrested, rolls away into a river. It is lost! it is irrecoverable! She has triumphed, but she must perish. Already she feels the flames mounting up which are to consume her, and she calls for water hastily﻿—not to deliver herself (for that is impossible), but, nobly forgetting her own misery, that she may prevent that destruction of her brother mortal which had been the original object for hazarding her own. Yet why go to Arabian fictions? Even in our daily life is exhibited, in proportions far more gigantic, that tendency to swell and amplify itself into mountains of darkness, which exists oftentimes in germs that are imperceptible. An error in human choice, an infirmity in the human will, though it were at first less than a mote, though it should swerve from the right line by an interval less than any thread

===Chinese===
《一千零一夜》（*Arabian Nights*）里流传着一个故事：一位公主，因漏数了石榴中的一颗籽，反倒促成了那桩她竭尽心力想要使之不可能实现的结局。她埋伏以待她所预见的那个结局。石榴胀大、绽开、迸裂；那些籽粒——她深知它们皆是祸根——被她急急吞下；但有一颗——仅仅一颗——未及截住，滚进了一条河里。它丢失了！再无法追回了！她胜利了，然而她必得灭亡。她已感到那注定吞噬她的火焰节节升腾，于是急促索水——并非为自救（那已是绝无可能的了），而是高尚地忘却自身的苦难，为的是阻止她那同为凡人的弟兄的毁灭——那原是她当初甘冒己身之险的初衷。然而，何必远求阿拉伯的故事？即便在我们的日常生活里，也展示着那种倾向——以远为浩大的比例膨胀、扩增为黑暗的崇山——而它往往潜存于无从察觉的胚芽之中。人类抉择中的一处谬误，人类意志中的一处孱弱，纵使其初起之时尚不及一粒微尘，纵使其偏离正道的间距，比任何一缕丝线还要细微

---

===Original===
> “That ever spider twisted from her womb,”

sometimes begins to swell, to grow, to widen its distance rapidly, travels off into boundless spaces remote from the true centre, spaces incalculable and irretraceable, until hope seems extinguished and return impossible. Such was the course of my own opium career. Such is the history of human errors every day. Such was the original sin of the Greek theories on Deity, which could not have been healed but by putting off their own nature, and kindling into a new principle﻿—absolutely undiscoverable, as I contend, for the Grecian intellect.
Oftentimes an echo goes as it were to sleep: the series of reverberations has died away. Suddenly a second series awakens: this subsides, then a third wakens up. So of actions done in youth. After great tumults all is quieted. You dream that they are over. In a moment, in the twinkling of an eye, on some fatal morning in middle-life the far-off consequences come back upon you. And you say to yourself, “Oh, Heaven, if I had fifty lives this crime would reappear, as Pelion upon Ossa!” So was it with my affection. Left to natural peace, I might have conquered it: *Verschmerzeon.* To charm it down by the mere suffering of grief, to hush it by endurance, that was the natural policy﻿—that was the natural process. But behold! A new form of sorrow arises, and the two multiply together. And the worm which was beginning to fall asleep is roused again to pestilential fierceness.

===Chinese===
> 「蛛母自其腹中所捻出的任何一缕丝，」

它有时却开始膨胀、滋长，迅速拉开自己的距离，漂离而去，进入远离真正中心的无限空间——无从测算、无从追溯的空间——直到希望仿佛熄灭，回归成为不可能。我自己的鸦片生涯，所行的正是这样一段历程。人类的种种谬误，天天都在上演着这样的历史。希腊人关于神性的诸种理论，其原罪亦复如此：除非脱去它们自身的本性、燃起为一种新的本原，那原罪便无从医治——而这一新的本原，依我之见，之于希腊人的才智乃是绝对无从发现的。
回声往往仿佛入睡：那一连串的回响已然止息。忽然，第二串苏醒过来：这又平息下去，于是第三串又醒了。青年时代所行的种种，情形亦是如此。大的纷扰过后，一切都归于平静。你梦以为它们已然过去。转瞬之间，在眨眼之际，在中年某个命定的清晨，那遥远的后果重新落到你身上。你对自己说：「哦，苍天哪，纵使我有五十条性命，这桩罪孽也仍会卷土重来，如皮利翁山叠压于奥萨山上（Pelion upon Ossa）！」我的情爱，情形也正是这样。倘任其安处于自然的平静，我或许本可克服它：*Verschmerzeon*（德语，谓以忍受将之消解）。凭哀伤的单纯受苦将它镇伏，凭坚忍使它沉寂——那本是自然的方略——那本是自然的过程。然而，看哪！一种新的悲恸之形崛起了，二者便相与孳生。那条正待入睡的虫豸，又被惊起，燃起瘟疫般的凶焰。

## The Solitude of Childhood / 童年的孤独

===Original===
As nothing which is impassioned escapes the eye of poetry, neither has this escaped it—that there is, or may be, through solitude, "sublime attractions of the grave." But even poetry has not perceived that these attractions may arise for a child. Not, indeed, a passion for the grave *as* the grave—from *that* a child revolts; but a passion for the grave as the portal through which it may recover some heavenly countenance, mother or sister, that has vanished. Through solitude this passion may be exalted into a frenzy like a nympholepsy. At first, when in childhood we find ourselves torn away from the lips that we could hang on forever, we throw out our arms in vain struggles to snatch at them, and pull them back again. But when we have felt for a time how hopeless is that effort, and that they cannot come to *us*, we desist from that struggle, and next we whisper to our hearts, Might not we go to *them*?

Such in principle and origin was the famous *Dulce Domum*48 of the English schoolboy. Such is the *Heimweh* (homesickness) of the German and Swiss soldier in foreign service. Such is the passion of the Calenture. Doubtless, reader, you have seen it described. The poor sailor is in tropical latitudes; deep, breathless calms have prevailed for weeks. Fever and delirium are upon him. Suddenly from his restless hammock he starts up; he will fret no longer in darkness; he ascends upon deck. How motionless are the deeps! How vast—how sweet are these shining *zaarrahs* of water! He gazes, and slowly under the blazing scenery of his brain the scenery of his eye unsettles. The waters are swallowed up; the seas have disappeared. Green fields appear, a silent dell, and a pastoral cottage. Two faces appear—are at the door—sweet female faces, and behold they beckon him. "Come to us!" they seem to say. The picture rises to his wearied brain like a *sanctus* from the choir of a cathedral, and in the twinkling of an eye, stung to madness by the cravings of his heart, the man is overboard. He is gone—he is lost for this world; but if he missed the arms of the lovely women—wife and sister—whom he sought, assuredly he has settled into arms that are mightier and not less indulgent.

===Chinese===
凡属深挚激越之情，无一能逃过诗的眼目；这一层同样不曾逃过——那就是，经由孤独，会有、或可能会有「坟墓的崇高吸引」。然而就连诗也不曾察觉，这种吸引竟能在一个孩子身上生起。诚然，那不是对坟墓*之为*坟墓的激情——*那*是孩子所畏避的；而是对坟墓作为一道门户的激情，经由这道门户，他或可重又迎回某张属天的面容，母亲或姊姊，那张已经消逝的面容。经由孤独，这种激情可以被激扬为一种癫狂，近似山泽女神附体的迷狂（nympholepsy）。起初，当我们在童年发觉自己被人从那双我们本可以永远依偎的唇边硬生生夺开时，我们张开双臂，徒然挣扎，想一把攫住他们，重新拉回到自己身边。但过了一段时间，我们已尝出这番努力何等无望，尝出他们不能到*我们*这里来，我们便止息了那番挣扎，随后便向自己心底悄声探问：何不能是*我们*到*他们*那里去？

英国学童那首著名的《Dulce Domum》（「甜美之家」）（48），其原理与根源正是如此。在异邦服役的德意志与瑞士士兵所怀的 *Heimweh*（思乡病）正是如此。谵妄热（calenture）的激情也正是如此。读者，你无疑见过对它的描写。可怜的水手正漂在热带的纬度；深沉而窒人的静风已绵延数周。热病与谵妄缠住了他。蓦地，他从躁动不宁的吊床上直起身来；他不肯再在黑暗中煎熬；他登上甲板。深渊何其凝定不动！这些闪耀于水面的 *zaarrahs*（细雨般的水光）何其浩渺——何其甘美！他凝望着，渐渐地，在他脑中灼燃的景物底下，眼中的景物涣然消散了。海水被吞没；诸海不见了。绿野显现，一方幽寂的溪谷，一座田园的茅舍。两副面容显现——就在门边——甜美的女子面容，看哪，她们在向他招手。「到我们这里来！」她们仿佛这样说。这幅图景升上他倦极的脑际，如同从大教堂的唱诗班中升起的一曲圣咏（*sanctus*）；而一眨眼之间，那人已被自己心底的渴念刺成癫狂，纵身翻入海中。他去了——他已失落于这个世界；然而，纵使他错过了他所寻访的那两副可爱的臂膀——妻子与姊姊——他确凿无疑地已安顿进另一些臂膀之中，那是更雄健的臂膀，而其温存并不稍减。

===Original===
I, young as I was, had one feeling not learned from books, and that *could* not have been learned from books, the deepest of all that connect themselves with natural scenery. It is the feeling which in "The Hart-leap Well" of Wordsworth, in his "Danish Boy," and other exquisite poems is brought out, viz., the breathless, mysterious, Pan-like silence that haunts the noonday. If there were winds abroad, then I was roused myself into sympathetic tumults. But if this dead silence haunted the air, then the peace which was in nature echoed another peace which lay in graves, and I fell into a sick languishing for things which a voice from heaven seemed to say "*cannot* be granted."

There is a German superstition, which eight or ten years after I read, of the Erl-king and his daughter. The daughter had power to tempt infants away into the invisible world; but it is, as the reader understands, by collusion with some infirmity of sick desire for such worlds in the infant itself.

===Chinese===
至于我自己，虽则年少，却已怀有一种并非从书本学来的情感——那也*断然*不是书本所能教的——在一切同自然景物相系连的情感之中，它是最深沉的一种。这就是华兹华斯（Wordsworth）在《鹿跳泉》（The Hart-leap Well）里、在他的《丹麦少年》（The Danish Boy）以及其他绝妙诗篇中所烘托出的那种情感，也就是那种屏息的、神秘的、潘神（Pan）一般的静默，在正午时分盘桓不去。若四野有风游行，我自己也会被激荡起同感的骚动。可若这种死寂盘桓在空气里，那么自然之中的安宁便应和起蛰伏于坟墓之内的另一种安宁，我便堕入一种病恹的渴慕，渴慕那些事物——仿佛有天上的声音在说，那些事物*断然*不能蒙允。

德意志有一则迷信，说的是桤木王（Erl-king）与他的女儿——桤木王之女；那传说我在八年或十年之后才读到。这位女儿有一种力量，能把婴孩诱引到无形的世界里去；然而——读者自能领会——这须凭婴孩自身对这类世界所怀的某种病弱渴念的弱点、与之串通，方能奏效。

===Original===
> "Who is that rides through the forest so fast?"

It is a knight who carries his infant upon his saddlebow. The Erl-king's daughter rides by his side; and, in words audible only when she means them to be heard, she says:

> "If thou wilt, dear baby, with me go away,
> We will see a fine show, we will play a fine play."

===Chinese===
> 「是谁骑着马儿，这般疾驰地穿过森林？」

那是一位骑士，鞍桥上抱带着他的婴孩。桤木王之女骑行在他身旁；她开口说话，那些话语唯有在她意欲让人听见时方能听见：

> 「亲爱的宝贝，你若肯跟我走，
> 好景致看不尽，好戏文玩不休。」

===Original===
That sounds lovely to my ears. Oh yes, that collusion with dim sleeping infancy is lovely to me; but I was too advanced in intellect to have been tempted by *such* temptations. Still there was a perilous attraction for me in worlds that slept and rested; and if the Erl-king's daughter had revealed herself to my perceptions, there was one "show" that she might have promised which would have wiled me away with her into the dimmest depths of the mightiest and remotest forests.

===Chinese===
这在我耳中听来是可爱的。哦，是的，同蒙昧酣睡的婴孩之间的这种串通，在我听来是可爱的；可我那时心智已开得太进，不致被*这样的*诱惑所诱动。然而，在那些沉睡着、安息着的世界里，于我总有一种危险的引力；倘若桤木王之女曾向我的知觉显现她自己，原有一种「景致」是她满可以许诺于我的——那景致会将我诱引而去，随她一同没入那最雄伟、最辽远的森林的至深至暗的幽处。

## Vision of Life / 生命的幻象

===Original===
Upon me, as upon others scattered thinly by tens and twenties over every thousand years, fell too powerfully and too early the vision of life. The horror of life mixed itself already in earliest youth with the heavenly sweetness of life; that grief which one in a hundred has sensibility enough to gather from the sad retrospect of life in its closing stage for *me* shed its dews as a prelibation upon the fountains of life whilst yet sparkling to the morning sun. I saw from afar and from before what I was to see from behind. Is this the description of an early youth passed in the shades of gloom? No; but of a youth passed in the divinest happiness. And, if the reader has (which so few have) the passion without which there is no reading of the legend and superscription upon man’s brow, if he is not (as most are) deafer than the grave to every *deep* note that sighs upwards from the Delphic caves of human life, he will know that the rapture of life (or anything which by approach can merit that name) does not arise, unless as perfect music arises, music of Mozart or Beethoven, by the confluence of the mighty and terrific discords with the subtle concords. Not by contrast, or as reciprocal foils, do these elements act—which is the feeble conception of many—but by union. They are the sexual forces in music: “male and female created he them”; and these mighty antagonists do not put forth their hostilities by repulsion, but by deepest attraction.

===Chinese===
同每一千年间仅以十数、二十数之数零星散布于世的少数人一样，生命的幻象（the vision of life）也降临于我——降临得过于强猛，也过于早了。生命的恐怖，早在稚龄之初，便已与生命那属天的甘甜相互掺杂；那种悲怆——一百人之中仅有一人具备足够的敏感，能从生命垂暮之境的凄然回望里将它采撷到手——之于*我*，却早在生命之泉还迎着朝阳熠熠闪烁的时节，便已把它的露滴当作先尝的祭酒（prelibation）洒落泉上。凡我日后须自后方回望方能得见的种种，我从远方、自前方，便已预先看见。这是在描述一段于阴翳幽暗之中度过的早年么？不是；它描述的，是一段在最神圣的幸福之中度过的青春。而读者倘具有那份激情（具此者何其稀少）——没有它，便无从读出人类额上的铭文与题字；倘他不似大多数人那样，比坟墓更其沉聋，听不见从人类生活的德尔斐洞窟（Delphic caves）中向上叹息的每一个*深沉*的音符——他便会懂得：生命的极乐（或任何凭其近似而差可配得上这名称的东西）之得以生发，恰如完美的音乐之得以生发——莫扎特（Mozart）或贝多芬（Beethoven）的音乐——必赖雄浑骇人的不协和音与精微的和协音二者之交汇。这些元素并非借对比、或作为相互的陪衬而发生作用——那是许多人的浅薄之见——而是借合一。它们正是音乐中的两性之力：「有男有女，是他造了他们」；而这些强大的对峙者并不以排斥施展它们的敌意，而是以最深的吸引。

---

===Original===
As “in today already walks tomorrow,” so in the past experience of a youthful life may be seen dimly the future. The collisions with alien interests or hostile views of a child, boy, or very young man, so insulated as each of these is sure to be—those aspects of opposition which such a person *can* occupy—are limited by the exceedingly few and trivial lines of connection along which he is able to radiate any essential influence whatever upon the fortunes or happiness of others. Circumstances may magnify his importance for the moment; but, after all, any cable which he carries out upon other vessels is easily slipped upon a feud arising. Far otherwise is the state of relations connecting an adult or responsible man with the circles around him as life advances. The network of these relations is a thousand times more intricate, the jarring of these intricate relations a thousand times more frequent, and the vibrations a thousand times harsher which these jarrings diffuse. This truth is felt beforehand, misgivingly and in troubled vision, by a young man who stands upon the threshold of manhood. One earliest instinct of fear and horror would darken his spirit if it could be revealed to itself and self-questioned at the moment of birth: a second instinct of the same nature would again pollute that tremulous mirror if the moment were as punctually marked as physical birth is marked which dismisses him finally upon the tides of absolute self-control. A dark ocean would seem the total expanse of life from the first; but far darker and more appalling would seem that inferior and second chamber of the ocean which called him away forever from the direct accountability of others. Dreadful would be the morning which should say, “Be thou a human child incarnate”; but more dreadful the morning which should say, “Bear thou henceforth the sceptre of thy self-dominion through life, and the passion of life!” Yes, dreadful would be both; but without a basis of the dreadful there is no perfect rapture. It is in part through the sorrow of life, growing out of dark events, that this basis of awe and solemn darkness slowly accumulates. *That* I have illustrated. But, as life expands, it is more through the *strife* which besets us, strife from conflicting opinions, positions, passions, interests, that the funereal ground settles and deposits itself which sends upward the dark lustrous brilliancy through the jewel of life, else revealing a pale and superficial glitter. Either the human being must suffer and struggle, as the price of a more searching vision, or his gaze must be shallow and without intellectual revelation.

===Chinese===
正如「明日已行走于今日之中」，在青春生涯的既往经验之中，也可朦胧窥见未来。孩童、少年或极年轻的男子，与异己的利害或敌对的观点之间所发生的碰撞——此三者中的每一个都注定是如此隔绝的——其*所能*占据的对抗形态，实为大受局限：他赖以向他人的际遇或幸福辐射任何实质影响的联结线路，稀少而微末之至。境遇或许会一时夸大他的重要性；但归根结底，他系向他船的任何缆索，一旦衅端骤起，都极易被解开脱弃。及至生命向前推进，一个成年人或身负重责的人与其周身诸圈层相维系的关系状态，便全然是另一番光景。这张关系之网的繁复要胜过千倍，这些繁复关系之间的龃龉要频繁千倍，而这些龃龉所扩散开的震颤，也要刺耳千倍。伫立于成年门槛上的年轻人，对此真理早有预感——怀着不祥的疑虑，在纷乱的视象之中预先感到。有一种最原初的恐惧与惊骇之本能，倘若在降生之顷它得向自身显露、得自审自问，便会使其灵魂蒙上阴影；而同类的第二种本能，倘若那个时刻也如肉身的降生一般被精确地标出——那便是将他最终遣送上绝对自制之潮流的时刻——也将再度玷污那面颤动的明镜。从一开始，一片黑暗的海洋便仿佛是生命的全部幅员；而那海洋更深一层的第二重内室——正是它召唤他永远离去，不复直接受他人的问责——便显得更暗、也更可怖。那宣告「愿你降生为一个血肉的人间孩童」的清晨，已然可怖；而那宣告「愿你自今而后，终生荷负你自我主宰的权杖，并荷负生命的激情！」的清晨，则更为可怖。是的，二者皆可怖；但没有可怖者作根基，便没有完美的极乐。这份敬畏与肃穆之暗的根基，一部分正是经由生命的悲怆——从幽暗的事变中生长出来的悲怆——缓缓积聚而成。*这一点*，我已有所阐发。然而，随着生命的展开，更多地倒是经由那困扰着我们的*纷争*——意见、立场、激情、利害彼此冲突的纷争——那葬仪般的地层方始沉降、淀积，也正是它透过生命这块宝石，向上透出幽暗而莹泽的光华；若无它，宝石所显露的便只是苍白而浮浅的闪光。人要么必须受苦、挣扎，以此为代价换取一种更深透的视界；要么，他的目光便必得肤浅，而无从获得智性的启示。

## Who Is This Woman That Beckoneth and Warneth Me from the Place Where She Is, and in Whose Eyes Is Woeful Remembrance? I Guess Who She Is / 这个向我招手示意、以凄然追忆的目光警醒我的女人是谁——我猜到她是谁

===Original===
In my dreams were often prefigurements of my future, as I could not but read the signs. What man has not some time in dewy morn, or sequestered eve, or in the still night-watches, when deep sleep falleth on other men but visiteth not his weary eyelids—what man, I say, has not some time hushed his spirit and questioned with himself whether some things seen or obscurely felt, were not anticipated as by mystic foretaste in some far halcyon time, postnatal or antenatal he knew not; only assuredly he knew that for him past and present and future merged in one awful moment of lightning revelation. Oh, spirit that dwelleth in man, how subtle are *thy* revelations; how deep, how delirious the raptures thou canst inspire; how poignant the stings with which thou canst pierce the heart; how sweet the honey with which thou assuagest the wound; how dark the despairs and accusings that lie behind thy curtains, and leap upon us like lightning from the cloud, with the sense as of some heavenly blazoning, and oftentimes carry us beyond ourselves!

===Chinese===
我的梦中，常常出现对我未来的预先摹形，而我不能不解读那些征兆。有什么人，不曾有时在露重的清晨，或在幽僻的黄昏，或在寂静的夜间更漏里——当酣睡降落在旁人身上，却独独不来临他疲惫的眼睑——有什么人，我说，不曾有时屏敛心神，向自己探问：某些见过或隐约感到的事物，岂不是早在某个遥远的宁谧岁月里，便已如同凭了一种神秘的先尝而被预先领略？那岁月在生后抑或生前，他无从知晓；唯有这一点他确凿知道：对他而言，过去、现在与未来，已在电光启示的那同一个骇人刹那之中融为一体。哦，居于人内的灵啊，*你*的启示是何等精微；你所能激发的狂喜是何等深沉、何等谵妄；你借以刺穿人心的那些毒刺是何等辛辣；你用来抚敷伤口的那蜜是何等甘甜；藏在你帷幔之后的那些绝望与控诉又是何等幽暗——它们如云中闪电一般向我们扑来，携着仿佛上天昭彰宣示的感觉，并且往往把我们带得超出我们自身之外！

===Original===
It is a sweet morning in June, and the fragrance of the roses is wafted towards me as I move—for I am walking in a lawny meadow, still wet with dew—and a wavering mist lies over the distance. Suddenly it seems to lift, and out of the dewy dimness emerges a cottage, embowered with roses and clustering clematis; and the hills, in which it is set like a gem, are tree-clad, and rise billowy behind it, and to the right and to the left are glistening expanses of water. Over the cottage there hangs a halo, as if clouds had but parted there. From the door of that cottage emerges a figure, the countenance full of the trepidation of some dread woe feared or remembered. With waving arm and tearful uplifted face the figure first beckons me onward, and then, when I have advanced some yards, frowning, warns me away. As I still continue to advance, despite the warning, darkness falls: figure, cottage, hills, trees, and halo fade and disappear; and all that remains to me is the look on the face of her that beckoned and warned me away. I read that glance as by the inspiration of a moment. We had been together; together we had entered some troubled gulf; struggled together, suffered together. Was it as lovers torn asunder by calamity? was it as combatants forced by bitter necessity into bitter feud, when we only, in all the world, yearned for peace together? Oh, what a searching glance was that which she cast on me! as if she, being now in the spiritual world, abstracted from flesh, remembered things that I could not remember. Oh, how I shuddered as the sweet sunny eyes in the sweet sunny morning of June—the month that was my “angelical”; half spring, yet with summer dress, that to me was very “angelical”—seemed reproachfully to challenge in me recollections of things passed thousands of years ago (old indeed, yet that were made new again for us, because now first it was that we met again). Oh, heavens! it came over me as doth the raven over the infected house, as from a bed of violets sweeps the saintly odour of corruption. What a glimpse was thus revealed! glory in despair, as of that gorgeous vegetation that hid the sterilities of the grave in the tropics of that summer long ago; of that heavenly beauty which slept side by side within my sister’s coffin in the month of June; of those saintly swells that rose from an infinite distance—I know not whether to or from my sister. Could this be a memorial of that nature? Are the nearer and more distant stages of life thus dimly connected, and the connection hidden, but suddenly revealed for a moment?

===Chinese===
那是六月里一个甜美的清晨，我行走之际，玫瑰的芬芳向我飘送而来——因为我正走在一片如茵的草甸上，露水未干——远方浮着一层摇曳不定的薄雾。蓦然间，那雾仿佛抬升起来，自带露的迷蒙中浮现出一座村舍，玫瑰花与团团簇簇的铁线莲把它环拥如花亭；群山遍体披着林木，如波涛般在屋后隆起，村舍便似宝石一般镶嵌在群山之中，左右两侧则是粼粼闪光的水面。村舍上方悬着一轮光晕，仿佛云朵恰恰只在此处分开。自那村舍的门中现出一个身影，面容上满是战栗，那战栗缘于某种可怖的悲哀——或是正被惧怕的，或是已被追忆的。那身影挥动着手臂，仰起含泪的脸，先向我招手，唤我前行；随后，当我前进了几码，又蹙起眉头，警醒我退开。尽管有那警示，我仍继续前行，于是黑暗降落：身影、村舍、山峦、树木与光晕，俱都淡去而消失；留存于我的，唯有那个招手示意又警醒我退开的人，她面容上的那一种神情。我解读那一瞥，仿佛凭着一瞬间的灵感。我们曾经同在；我们曾一同踏入某个纷扰的渊薮；一同挣扎，一同受苦。莫非我们是被灾祸生生拆散的恋人？莫非我们是被苦涩的必然逼入苦涩仇隙的争斗者——而在整个世界上，唯独我们两人，彼此渴念着同享和平？哦，她投在我身上的那一瞥，是怎样一种搜剔肺腑的目光！仿佛她如今既已身在灵界，脱卸了肉身，便记得着我所不能记起的事情。哦，当那双甜美的、阳光的眼睛——在六月那甜美而晴朗的清晨里——那个于我堪称「天使之月」的月份；半是春光，却已着上夏装，而那于我真是十分「天使般」——仿佛含着责备，向我心中追问起数千年前已然逝去之事的记忆时，我战栗得何等厉害！（那些事诚然古老，然而它们为我们重新变作了新的，因为此刻方才是我们重逢之始。）哦，天哪！那感觉临到我，正如渡鸦盘临染疫的宅邸，正如自一畦紫罗兰之上掠过的、腐朽的神圣气息。就这样显露出来的一瞥之中，含着何等景象！绝望之中的荣光——仿佛许久以前那个夏天的热带地带里，把坟墓的荒瘠遮掩起来的绚丽草木；仿佛六月里在我姊姊的棺木之内与她并肩长眠的那份属天的美；仿佛自无穷远处升起的那些神圣的波涌——我不知那波涌是朝向我姊姊，抑或自我姊姊而来。这难道会是那种本性的一个纪念么？生命中较近与较远的诸阶段，竟是这般隐隐相连，连结藏而不露，却会蓦然间显露一瞬么？

===Original===
This lady for years appeared to me in dreams; in that, considering the electric character of my dreams, and that they were far less like a lake reflecting the heavens than like the pencil of some mighty artist—Da Vinci or Michelangelo—that cannot copy in simplicity, but comments in freedom, while reflecting in fidelity, there was nothing to surprise. But a change in this appearance was remarkable. Oftentimes, after eight years had passed, she appeared in summer dawn at a window. It was a window that opened on a balcony. This feature only gave a distinction, a refinement, to the aspect of the cottage—else all was simplicity. Spirit of Peace, dove-like dawn that slept upon the cottage, ye were not broken by any participation in my grief and despair! Forever the vision of that cottage was renewed. Did I roam in the depths of sweet pastoral solitudes in the West, with the tinkling of sheep-bells in my ears, a rounded hillock, seen vaguely, would shape itself into a cottage; and at the door my monitory, regretful Hebe would appear. Did I wander by the seashore, one gently-swelling wave in the vast heaving plain of waters would suddenly transform itself into a cottage, and I, by some involuntary inward impulse, would in fancy advance toward it.

===Chinese===
这位女士，有多年之久在我的梦中显现；就这一点而言，想到我的梦那带电般的性质，想到它们远不像一泓映照天光的湖水，而更像某位巨匠的画笔——达·芬奇（Da Vinci）或米开朗基罗（Michelangelo）——那画笔不能以单纯摹写为事，而是一面忠实映照，一面纵笔评注，其中原没有什么可惊讶之处。但这显现之中的一处变化却惹人注目。常常，在八年过去之后，她会在夏日的黎明显现于一扇窗前。那是一扇开向阳台的窗。唯有这一处特征，给那村舍的形貌平添了一种出众、一种精雅——此外一切都还单纯。和平之灵啊，栖宿在那村舍之上的鸽子般的黎明啊，你们不曾因分尝我的悲哀与绝望而被搅破分毫！那村舍的幻象，永远在更新重现。倘若我漫游在西方甜美田园幽独的深处，耳中响着羊铃的叮咚，一座望之朦胧的浑圆小丘，便会自行塑成一座村舍；而门前，我那位示警而怀憾的赫柏（Hebe）便会显现。倘若我漫步在滨海之侧，浩渺起伏的水之平野上一道徐徐隆起的浪，便会蓦地幻变为一座村舍；而我，凭着某种不由自主的内在冲动，便会在想象中向它走去。

===Original===
Ah, reader, you will think this which I am going to say too near, too holy, for recital. But not so. The deeper a woe touches me in heart, so much the more am I urged to recite it. The world disappears: I see only the grand relics of a world—memorials of a love that has departed, has been—the record of a sorrow that is, and has its greyness converted into verdure—monuments of a wrath that has been reconciled, of a wrong that has been atoned for—convulsions of a storm that has gone by. What I am going to say is the most like a superstitious thing that I ever shall say. And I have reason to think that every man who is not a villain once in his life must be superstitious. It is a tribute which he pays to human frailty, which tribute if he will not pay, which frailty if he will not share, then also he shall not have any of its strength.

The face of this monitory Hebe haunted me for some years in a way that I must faintly attempt to explain. It is little to say that it was the sweetest face, with the most peculiar expression of sweetness, that I had ever seen: that was much, but that was earthly. There was something more terrific, believe me, than this; yet that was not the word: terror looks to the future; and this perhaps did, but not primarily. Chiefly it looked at some unknown past, and was for that reason awful; yes, awful—that was the word.

===Chinese===
啊，读者，你会以为我下面要说的话过于切近、过于神圣，不宜宣之于口。然而并非如此。悲哀触及我心愈深，我便愈是被催迫着要把它陈说出来。世界隐没了：我看见的只是一个世界残留下的宏伟遗物——一段已然逝去、却确曾存在过的爱的诸般纪念——一桩尚在的悲哀的记录，它那灰白已被点化成青翠——一场已然和解的震怒、一桩已然抵偿的冤屈的纪念碑——一场已然过去的暴风雨的痉挛。我即将说出的话，是我平生所说之中最近乎迷信的话。而我有理由认为，每一个不是恶棍的人，一生之中总有一度必定要迷信一回。这是他向人性弱点所纳的贡赋；这份贡赋他若不肯纳，这弱点他若不肯分担，那么他也就不得分享它丝毫的力量。

这位示警的赫柏的那张面容，有好几年缠绕着我，其缠绕的方式，我只能隐隐约约试着说明。若只说我平生所见从未有过一张那般甜美的面容、那般独特的甜美神情，那还说得太少：那已是很多，但那仍是尘世的。请相信我，其中还有比这更可怖的东西；然而「可怖」也还不是那个词：恐怖（terror）望向未来；这一份或许也望向未来，却非其本旨。它主要是望向某段无人知晓的过去，并因此而是可畏的（awful）；是的，可畏——那才是那个词。

===Original===
Thus, on any of those heavenly sunny mornings, that now are buried in an endless grave, did I, transported by no human means, enter that cottage, and descend to that breakfast-room, my earliest salute was to her, that ever, as the look of pictures do, with her eyes pursued me round the room, and oftentimes with a subtle checking of grief, as if great sorrow had been or would be hers. And it was, too, in the sweet Maytime. Oh yes; she was but as if she had been—as if it were her original … chosen to have been the aurora of a heavenly clime; and then suddenly she was as one of whom, for some thousand years, Paradise had received no report; then, again, as if she entered the gates of Paradise not less innocent; and, again, as if she could not enter; and some blame—but I knew not what blame—was mine; and now she looked as though broken with a woe that no man could read, as she sought to travel back to her early joy—yet no longer a joy that is sublime in innocency, but a joy from which sprung abysses of memories polluted into anguish, till her tears seemed to be suffused with drops of blood. All around was peace and the deep silence of untroubled solitude; only in the lovely lady was a sign of horror, that had slept, under deep ages of frost, in her heart, and now rose, as with the rushing of wings, to her face. Could it be supposed that one life—so pitiful a thing—was what moved her care? Oh no; it was, or it seemed, as if this poor wreck of a life happened to be that one which determined the fate of some thousand others. Nothing less; nothing so abject as one poor fifty years—nothing less than a century of centuries could have stirred the horror that rose to her lovely lips, as once more she waved me away from the cottage.

Oh, reader, five years after I saw that sweet face in reality—saw it in the flesh; saw that pomp of womanhood; saw that cottage; saw a thousand times that lovely domicile that heard the cooing of the solitary dove in the solitary morning; saw the grace of childhood and the shadows of graves that lay, like creatures asleep, in the sunshine; saw, also, the horror, somehow realized as a shadowy reflection from myself, which warned me off from that cottage, and which still rings through the dreams of five-and-twenty years.

===Chinese===
就这样，在那些如今已葬入无尽坟墓的属天晴晨里的任何一晨，我——不经任何人间的运载——走进那座村舍，下到那间早餐室；我最先的致意总是献给她，而她，一如画中人顾盼的神情，总用她的目光随着我绕室逡巡，且往往带着一缕隐然抑制的悲哀，仿佛极大的忧伤曾经是、或将会是她的。而这也正是在甜美的五月时节。哦，是的；她起初仿佛一如她本来所是——仿佛那本是她的原样……蒙拣选要做一方属天境域的曙光；随后蓦然间，她又成了这样一个人：关于她，天堂已千年不曾收到片纸音讯；继而，又仿佛她迈入天堂诸门时，清白并无稍减；再继而，又仿佛她终究不得其门而入；而某种咎责——但我不知是怎样的咎责——是在于我；此刻，当她力图跋涉回她早年的欢乐时，她看上去像是被一种无人能解读的悲哀折断了——那已不再是清白无邪之中崇高的欢乐，而是这样一种欢乐：自它涌生出记忆的深渊，那记忆已被玷污而成苦楚，直到她的泪水仿佛浸透了滴滴鲜血。四围皆是和平，皆是无扰孤寂的深沉静默；唯有在这位可爱的女士身上有一种恐怖的征象——那恐怖曾在她心中、在重重冰封的邃古岁月之下酣睡，此刻却如振翅疾冲一般，升上了她的面庞。难道可以设想，一条性命——如此可怜的一样东西——竟是她所关切所系的东西么？哦，不是的；那情形是——或仿佛是——这一条性命的可怜残骸，碰巧正是那决定着其余千百人性命之命运的那一条。能鼓动那恐怖的，绝没有什么会卑微到仅仅可怜的五十年这般田地——必得整整一个诸世纪的世纪，方才鼓动得起那升上她可爱唇际的恐怖——那时，她再一次挥手，令我离开那座村舍。

哦，读者，五年之后，我在现实中见到了那张甜美的面容——在肉身之中见到了它；见到了那女性的雍容华彩；见到了那座村舍；千百次地见到了那处可爱的居所——它在孤寂的清晨，听那孤单的鸽子咕咕低语；见到了童年的秀雅与坟墓的暗影，那些暗影像睡熟的小生灵一般卧在阳光里；也见到了那恐怖——它不知怎的实现出来，成了从我自己身上投下的一个暗淡映像，正是它把我从那座村舍前警告开去，并且至今仍在二十五个年头的梦境中铮然鸣响。

---

===Original===
The general sentiment or sense of preexistence, of which this *Suspiria* may be regarded as one significant and affecting illustration, had this record in the outset of the *Reminiscences of Wordsworth*:

> “Oh, sense of mysterious preexistence, by which, through years, in which as yet a stranger to those valleys of Westmoreland, I viewed myself as a phantom self—a second identity projected from my own consciousness, and already living amongst them—how was it, and by what prophetic instinct, that already I said to myself oftentimes, when chasing daydreams along the pictures of these wild mountainous labyrinths, which as yet I had not traversed, ‘Here, in some distant year, I shall be shaken with love, and there with stormiest grief and regret’? Whence was it that sudden revelations came upon me, like the drawings up of a curtain, and closing again as rapidly, of scenes that made the future heaven of my life? And how was it that in thought I *was*, and yet in reality *was not*, a denizen, already, in 1803, 1804, 1805, of lakes and forest lawns, which I never saw till 1807? and that, by a prophetic instinct of heart, I rehearsed and lived over, as it were, in vision those chapters of my life which have carried with them the weightiest burden of joy and sorrow, and by the margin of those very lakes and hills with which I prefigured this connection? and, in short, that for me, by a transcendent privilege, during the novitiate of my life, most truly I might say:

===Chinese===
这种关于先在（preexistence）的普遍情感或意识——《叹息集》（*Suspiria*）中的这一篇，或可视为它一个意味深长而动人心魄的例证——在《华兹华斯回忆录》（*Reminiscences of Wordsworth*）的开篇处，曾有这样一段记载：

> 「哦，神秘先在的感觉啊——凭着它，在我尚且是威斯特摩兰（Westmoreland）诸山谷间一个陌生客的岁月里，我把自己看作一个幻影般的自我，一重自我自身意识中投射出来的第二身份，并且已经生活于那些山谷之间——这究竟是怎么回事？是凭了怎样一种先知般的直觉，以至于早在那时，当我沿着这些狂野山峦迷宫的图景——那些我尚未亲历的迷宫——追逐白日梦时，便已屡屡对自己说：『在这遥远的某一年，我将在何处为爱所震撼，在彼处为最狂暴的悲哀与悔恨所震撼』？又是从何而来，那些蓦然的启示降临于我——如同一幅帷布倏然拉起，又同样迅疾地阖上——启示出的景象，正是构成我此生未来天堂的那些景象？又是怎么回事，早在 1803、1804、1805 年，在思想中我*已然是*、而在现实中我*尚不是*那些湖泽与林间草坪的一位居民——那些湖山我直到 1807 年方才亲见？又是怎么回事，凭着一种心灵的先知直觉，我仿佛在幻视之中预演并重活过我此生的那些篇章——那些携带着至重悲欢之负荷的篇章——而且恰是在那些湖山之畔，我正是借它们预画出这种连结？总而言之，又是怎么回事，于我而言，凭了一种超凡的特权，在我此生的见习期里，我竟可以极其真确地说：
